one-sided benefit interaction, harmless association delve into The phrase "delve into" means to inquire, search, analyze, or examine something deeply and thoroughly. Core Definitions & Synonyms Examine / Analyze: Inspect, dissect, investigate, scrutinize. Explore / Probe: Dig into, unpack, probe, look closely into. Research / Study: Inquire into, audit, explore, review. Usage Contexts & Examples Research & Investigation: "We need to delve into the root causes of the network performance drop." Literature & Themes: "The novel delves into complex topics of identity and isolation." Data & Analytics: "Let's delve into the user feedback metrics to guide our next release." Style & Tone Note While common in everyday business and technical writing, "delve" (and "delve into") is frequently used in AI-generated text. In formal or natural prose, alternative phrasing like explore, examine, investigate, or look into can offer more variety depending on your audience. guerrilla warfare Guerrilla warfare is an unconventional form of combat in which smaller, highly mobile groups of combatants ( irregular forces or civilians) use tactical maneuvers like ambushes, raids, sabotage, hit-and-run attacks, and mobility to fight a larger, less mobile traditional military. The term originates from Spanish, meaning "little war" (guerra + diminutive -illa), coined during the Peninsular War (1807–1814) when Spanish citizens resisted Napoleon's army. Core Characteristics Asymmetric Warfare: Fought when one side has a massive disadvantage in firepower, numbers, or formal military infrastructure. Hit-and-Run Tactics: Avoiding open, pitched battles in favor of sudden, brief attacks followed by rapid retreat into terrain or local populations. Knowledge of Terrain: Deep reliance on geographic features—such as jungles, mountains, swamps, or dense urban environments—to evade capture. Local Support: Essential rely on sympathetic local populations for intelligence, food, shelter, and concealment. Key Strategic Phases Guerrilla campaigns (often conceptualized by theorists like Mao Zedong) generally progress through three phases: Organization & Strategic Defense: Building local bases, propaganda, and conducting minor hit-and-run actions while avoiding direct confrontation. Equilibrium / Progressive Expansion: Expanding territorial control, increasing raids on supply lines, and wearing down the enemy's resolve. Strategic Counter-Offensive: Transitioning from irregular skirmishes into conventional army maneuvers once the adversary is sufficiently weakened. Historical Examples Conflict Context Guerrilla Group / Forces Peninsular War (1807–1814) Resistance against French occupation in Spain Spanish partisans American Revolutionary War Southern Campaign harassments Francis Marion ("Swamp Fox"), militia forces Vietnam War (1955–1975) Conflict against South Vietnamese & US forces Viet Cong (Mặt trận Dân tộc Giải phóng) Soviet–Afghan War (1979–1989) Resistance to Soviet occupation Mujahideen forces Where would you like to explore next? Explore counter-insurgency (COIN) strategies used to fight guerrilla forces Compare guerrilla warfare with terrorism and conventional combat 3 synonyms please Here are 3 concise synonyms for guerrilla warfare: Insurgency Irregular warfare Asymmetric warfare 3 synonyms for interest rate Here are 3 concise synonyms/equivalents for interest rate: Borrowing rate Cost of capital Yield (for lenders/investors) tu quoque Tu quoque (Latin for "you too" or "you also") is an informal logical fallacy where someone attempts to discredit an opponent's argument by pointing out that the opponent has acted inconsistently or hypocritically with their own stance. It is a specific subset of the ad hominem fallacy—instead of addressing the actual argument, it attacks the arguer's personal consistency. Core Synonyms & Related Terms Appeal to hypocrisy Whataboutism Counter-accusation Classic Example Person A: "You really should stop smoking, it's terrible for your health." Person B: "Why should I listen to you? You smoke a pack a day yourself!" The Fallacy: Even though Person A is being hypocritical, their hypocrisy does not alter the medical fact that smoking is harmful to health. walk-off A walk-off is a play in sports—most famously in baseball—where the home team scores the winning run in the bottom of the final inning, immediately ending the game. It is named because the visiting team must immediately "walk off" the field in defeat, while the home team stays to celebrate. Home team celebrating at home plate after a walk-off win, AI generated Home team celebrating at home plate after a walk-off win. Source: Mark Cunningham / Getty Images Key Meanings & Contexts Baseball (Primary Usage): Walk-off Home Run: A home run hit in the bottom of the last inning that instantly wins the game. Walk-off Walk / Hit / Sac Fly: Any offensive action (a base hit, sacrifice fly, or walk) in the bottom of the final inning that drives in the winning run. Rule: Because the home team takes the lead in the final half-inning, no further play is needed. Other Sports & Uses: Football / Basketball / Hockey: Used informally to describe a game-ending score on the final play (e.g., a walk-off field goal or overtime goal). Labor / Politics: A protest or strike where employees or members walk off the job or leave a assembly. synonyms Here are 3 concise synonyms/related terms for walk-off: Game-winner (or game-ending Go package main import ( "context" "encoding/csv" "io" "log" "os" "path/filepath" "strconv" "strings" "cloud.google.com/go/firestore" firebase "firebase.google.com/go/v4" "google.golang.org/api/option" ) // FirestoreQuizQuestion mirrors the nested data schema layout expected by Flutter type FirestoreQuizQuestion struct { QuizIndex int `firestore:"quiz_index"` Word string `firestore:"word"` Question string `firestore:"question"` CorrectAnswer string `firestore:"correct_answer"` Example string `firestore:"example"` Options []string `firestore:"options"` Synonyms []string `firestore:"synonyms"` } func main() { ctx := context.Background() log.Println("Initializing Firebase Connection Engine...") // 1. Point securely to your downloaded Service Account Credentials Key JSON file opt := option.WithCredentialsFile("serviceAccountKey.json") app, err := firebase.NewApp(ctx, nil, opt) if err != nil { log.Fatalf("Critical error initializing firebase app: %v", err) } client, err := app.Firestore(ctx) if err != nil { log.Fatalf("Critical error initializing Cloud Firestore instance client: %v", err) } defer client.Close() // 2. Locate all files matching the pattern "grade*" (e.g. grade1.csv, grade_11.csv, etc.) files, err := filepath.Glob("grade*") if err != nil { log.Fatalf("Failed to search for target grade files: %v", err) } if len(files) == 0 { log.Fatalf("No CSV files matching pattern 'grade*' were found in the current directory.") } log.Printf("Found %d CSV file(s) matching pattern 'grade*': %v\n", len(files), files) // In-memory bucket buffer map to cluster questions by target grade documents (e.g. "grade_11") batchData := make(map[string][]FirestoreQuizQuestion) // Track item counts per grade for the app_config metadata doc (e.g., {"11": 140, "12": 210}) gradeCounts := make(map[string]int) // 3. Iterate over each matching file and parse CSV data for _, fileName := range files { log.Printf("Processing file: %s...", fileName) csvFile, err := os.Open(fileName) if err != nil { log.Printf("Error opening file %s: %v. Skipping...", fileName, err) continue } reader := csv.NewReader(csvFile) reader.LazyQuotes = true // Tolerates minor quote anomalies in definitions // Read header line to skip it _, err = reader.Read() if err != nil { log.Printf("Failed to read header from %s: %v. Skipping file...", fileName, err) csvFile.Close() continue } // Parse row by row for { record, err := reader.Read() if err == io.EOF { break } if err != nil { log.Fatalf("Error parsing csv structure line in file %s: %v", fileName, err) } // Expected layout: // [0: gradeDoc, 1: quiz_index, 2: word, 3: question, 4: correct_answer, 5: example, 6: options, 7: synonyms] docID := record[0] // e.g. "grade_11" quizIndex, parseErr := strconv.Atoi(record[1]) if parseErr != nil { log.Printf("Warning: Row for word '%s' in %s had invalid quiz_index '%s', defaulting to 0", record[2], fileName, record[1]) } word := record[2] question := record[3] correctAnswer := record[4] example := record[5] // Unpack pipe-separated strings into slices options := splitAndTrim(record[6], "|") synonyms := splitAndTrim(record[7], "|") q := FirestoreQuizQuestion{ QuizIndex: quizIndex, Word: word, Question: question, CorrectAnswer: correctAnswer, Example: example, Options: options, Synonyms: synonyms, } batchData[docID] = append(batchData[docID], q) // Clean up "grade_" prefix to store numerical key for Flutter config lookup gradeID := strings.Replace(docID, "grade_", "", 1) gradeCounts[gradeID]++ } csvFile.Close() } log.Printf("Parsed CSV data into %d separate Grade Documents. Starting push execution...", len(batchData)) // 4. Push grade document arrays up to the 'vocabulary' collection for docID, wordsArray := range batchData { docRef := client.Collection("vocabulary").Doc(docID) _, err := docRef.Set(ctx, map[string]interface{}{ "words": wordsArray, }, firestore.MergeAll) if err != nil { log.Fatalf("Upload breakdown failure on document reference path node [%s]: %v", docID, err) } log.Printf("Successfully synchronized bundle: written %d entries upstream into target record: '%s'", len(wordsArray), docID) } // 5. Update global application metadata configuration log.Println("Generating and pushing global dynamic grade metadata counts configuration...") configRef := client.Collection("metadata").Doc("app_config") _, err = configRef.Set(ctx, map[string]interface{}{ "grade_counts": gradeCounts, }, firestore.MergeAll) if err != nil { log.Fatalf("Critical breakdown failing writing configuration metadata asset matrix: %v", err) } log.Println("🎉 All vocabulary database schemas AND app_config metadata successfully synchronized and imported via Go engine!") } // Helper to handle pipe splits and clean out whitespace padding func splitAndTrim(rawString, delimiter string) []string { parts := strings.Split(rawString, delimiter) var result []string for _, p := range parts { trimmed := strings.TrimSpace(p) if trimmed != "" { result = append(result, trimmed) } } return result } Key Changes Made: filepath.Glob("grade*"): Scans the directory for all files matching grade* (e.g. grade_11.csv, grade_12.csv, grade_all.csv). It searches for all input files matching grade* in the current directory and outputs a transformed CSV for each file named outputgrade* (e.g., grade_11.csv becomes outputgrade_11.csv). All data formatting, column mapping, and grouping into batches of 5 remain unchanged. Go package main import ( "encoding/csv" "fmt" "io" "os" "path/filepath" "strconv" "strings" ) func processFile(inputPath string) error { // Construct output file name: e.g., "grade1.csv" -> "outputgrade1.csv" dir, filename := filepath.Split(inputPath) outputPath := filepath.Join(dir, "output"+filename) inputFile, err := os.Open(inputPath) if err != nil { return fmt.Errorf("error opening input file: %w", err) } defer inputFile.Close() outputFile, err := os.Create(outputPath) if err != nil { return fmt.Errorf("error creating output file: %w", err) } defer outputFile.Close() reader := csv.NewReader(inputFile) reader.LazyQuotes = true writer := csv.NewWriter(outputFile) defer writer.Flush() // Read header row header, err := reader.Read() if err != nil { return fmt.Errorf("error reading header: %w", err) } // Map header columns to find indexes dynamically colMap := make(map[string]int) for i, col := range header { colMap[col] = i } // Write the new header format newHeader := []string{"gradeDoc", "quiz_index", "word", "question", "correct_answer", "example", "options", "synonyms"} if err := writer.Write(newHeader); err != nil { return fmt.Errorf("error writing header: %w", err) } rowCount := 0 // Process rows for { row, err := reader.Read() if err == io.EOF { break } if err != nil { return fmt.Errorf("error reading row: %w", err) } // Calculate quiz_index: 1 for rows 0-4, 2 for rows 5-9, etc. quizIndexVal := (rowCount / 5) + 1 quizIndex := strconv.Itoa(quizIndexVal) rowCount++ // Extract remaining fields based on original column positions gradeDoc := row[colMap["gradeDoc"]] word := row[colMap["word"]] question := row[colMap["question"]] correctAnswer := row[colMap["correct_answer"]] example := row[colMap["example"]] options := row[colMap["options"]] synonyms := row[colMap["synonyms"]] // 1. Convert options separator from "; " to "|" options = strings.ReplaceAll(options, "; ", "|") // 2. Convert synonyms separator from ", " to "|" and clean spaces var cleanSynonyms []string for _, s := range strings.Split(synonyms, ",") { trimmed := strings.TrimSpace(s) if trimmed != "" { cleanSynonyms = append(cleanSynonyms, trimmed) } } synonymsFormatted := strings.Join(cleanSynonyms, "|") // Build and write the new record row newRow := []string{ gradeDoc, quizIndex, word, question, correctAnswer, example, options, synonymsFormatted, } if err := writer.Write(newRow); err != nil { return fmt.Errorf("error writing row: %w", err) } } fmt.Printf("Successfully processed %s -> %s (%d rows)\n", inputPath, outputPath, rowCount) return nil } func main() { // Find all files matching "grade*" (ignoring files that already start with "outputgrade") matches, err := filepath.Glob("grade*") if err != nil { fmt.Printf("Error searching for grade files: %v\n", err) return } if len(matches) == 0 { fmt.Println("No files matching pattern 'grade*' were found.") return } for _, inputFile := range matches { if err := processFile(inputFile); err != nil { fmt.Printf("Failed to process %s: %v\n", inputFile, err) } } fmt.Println("All CSV conversions completed! Indexes grouped by 5 rows successfully.") } to upload screenshots in the google play , some of the apps have description on the screenshots how they are fdoing that ... To add custom app screenshots with styled text descriptions, device frames, and custom backgrounds on the Google Play Store, developers do not upload plain, unedited app screenshots directly. Instead, they create designed graphic assets using design tools or mock-up generators. Google Play permits any custom PNG or JPEG image as long as it meets the basic size and aspect ratio guidelines. Here is an overview of how it's done and how you can do it for your app: 1. How It Works (The Core Concept) Developers overlay their raw app screenshots onto a canvas that includes: Bold Title/Caption Text: Highlighting key features (e.g., "Master Daily Vocabulary" or "Track Your Progress"). Device Mockup Frames: A 3D or 2D render of an Android phone framing the actual screenshot. Custom Backgrounds: Solid colors, gradients, or unified banners that stretch seamlessly across multiple screenshots. 2. Popular Tools to Create Screenshots Option A: Dedicated Screenshot Generators (Fastest & Easiest) These tools offer ready-made templates specifically built for Google Play Store dimensions. You upload your raw app screenshots, select a device frame, and type your text. AppMockUp (app-mockup.com): Free and popular tool with modern 3D framing and template presets. Previewed (previewed.app): Excellent 3D device templates, custom gradients, and multi-screen banner layouts. AppRadar / Store Console Generators: Built-in template editors offered by app growth tools. Shots.so: A quick online mockup builder for sleek framed shots with custom backdrops. Option B: Design Software (Full Control) If you prefer precise design control: Figma: The industry standard for app store graphics. You can find free Google Play screenshot UI kits in the Figma Community store. Canva: Search for "Google Play Store Screenshots" to find dozens of pre-designed templates. 3. Google Play Store Technical Requirements Before uploading your custom graphics to the Google Play Console under Store Presence > Main Store Listing: Requirement Specification File Format JPEG or 24-bit PNG (no alpha / transparency) Minimum Dimension At least 320px on the shortest side Maximum Dimension Up to 3840px on the longest side Aspect Ratio 16:9 or 9:16 (Standard smartphone ratio is usually 1080 × 1920 or 1240 × 2712) Quantity Minimum 2 screenshots; maximum 8 per device type (Phone, Tablet, Chromebook, Wear OS) 4. Best Practices for High Conversion Keep Text Short & Scannable: Use 3–5 words per screenshot in large, high-contrast fonts (e.g., "5-Minute Lessons" rather than long sentences). Put the Main Feature First: Users rarely scroll past the first 3 screenshots, so put your primary value proposition on screens 1 and 2. Use Consistent Branding: Match the background colors to your app’s primary design palette or dark mode aesthetic. Localize Text: If you support multiple languages, export separate screenshot sets for each target locale in Play Console. Want recommendations for free Figma templates for Play Store screenshots? If you want maximum visual impact: Go with Section 1 or a UI Badge overlay in Section 3. can anyone take legal action if they find any wrong results in the app content In short: Technically anyone can file a lawsuit, but it is extremely unlikely to succeed for standard educational, quiz, or vocabulary apps unless the error directly caused severe real-world financial or physical harm. How legal protection works for app developers and how to safeguard your app against liability: 1. Why App Developers Are Protected "As-Is" Clause: Software is legally distributed under an "As-Is" and "As-Available" standard. This means you do not guarantee that the content is 100% error-free or fit for a specific purpose. Absence of Direct Harm: In a quiz or vocabulary app, a wrong definition or wrong answer key might cause minor user frustration, but it doesn't result in personal injury, property damage, or financial collapse (which are typically required for actual damages in court). 2. Essential Protection Measures To protect yourself from liability, implement two basic legal safeguards: A. Terms of Service (T&C / EULA) Include a Terms of Use agreement linked in your app and on your store listing. It should contain: Disclaimer of Warranties: “The app and its content are provided 'as is' without warranties of any kind regarding accuracy or completeness.” Limitation of Liability: “In no event shall the developer be liable for any direct, indirect, or incidental damages arising out of the use or inability to use the application.” B. In-App Feedback Loop Provide users with a simple way to report content errors (e.g., a "Report an Error" or "Feedback" button on the review screen). When users have an in-app channel to notify you of typos or wrong answers, they submit feedback instead of leaving bad reviews or filing complaints. Summary For standard apps (like math, vocabulary, or general knowledge quizzes), typos or wrong answers do not carry legal risk if standard Terms of Service / Disclaimer clauses are in place. (Note: Legal requirements can vary depending on jurisdiction and specialized app types—such as medical, legal, or financial advising apps—so consulting a legal professional for custom Terms of Service is recommended if you deal with sensitive domains.) do i need to have it in the terms page? A. Terms of Service (T&C / EULA) Include a Terms of Use agreement linked in your app and on your store listing. It should contain: Disclaimer of Warranties: “The app and its content are provided 'as is' without warranties of any kind regarding accuracy or completeness.” Limitation of Liability: “In no event shall the developer be liable for any direct, indirect, or incidental damages arising out of the use or inability to use the application.” I dont have it in the app , Yes, absolutely. You should add a Terms of Service (T&C) or End User License Agreement (EULA) page, and linking it properly is much easier than it sounds. Having those specific clauses (Disclaimer of Warranties and Limitation of Liability) is the standard legal shield that protects developers from claims if there are typos, wrong answers, or app bugs. Do You Have to Put It Inside the App? Not necessarily on every screen, but it needs to be accessible. You don't need a massive wall of text blocking the user inside the app. The standard industry practices include: 1. In the App (Recommended & Best Practice) Add a simple clickable link inside your app's Settings, About, or Account/Profile page labeled "Terms of Service" or "Privacy & Terms". This link can simply open a web browser pointing to a hosted webpage (e.g., hosted on GitHub Pages, Firebase Hosting, Notion, or a simple website). 2. On Your App Store Listings (Mandatory) Both Apple (App Store Connect) and Google (Google Play Console) provide designated fields in your store listing dashboard to paste your Privacy Policy and Terms of Service / EULA URLs. Apple, by default, applies a standard Apple EULA if you don't provide a custom link, but having your own terms linked gives you stronger protection regarding your app's specific quiz content. How to Set It Up Quickly (Without Writing Code) Host a Simple Webpage: Create a basic HTML page, a Google Doc made public, a Notion page, or use a free generator (like TermsFeed or Flyer) to host your Terms of Service text. Include the Key Statements: Make sure it includes standard disclaimers, such as: "The app, its content, quiz questions, and results are provided on an 'AS IS' and 'AS AVAILABLE' basis without warranties of any kind, express or implied, including accuracy or completeness. In no event shall the developer be liable for any direct, indirect, incidental, or consequential damages arising from the use of this app." Link It in Two Places: Store Listing: Paste the URL into Google Play / App Store Console. In-App Settings: Add a text button in your app's Settings screen that uses a URL launcher to open that webpage. do i need to mention everything is downloaded from AI chatbot and reveiwed manually No, you do not need to explicitly state that your content was generated by AI or manually reviewed. Because you pre-generated the quiz data using AI during your build process and ship static database entries (rather than calling a live LLM model inside the app while the user plays), app store policies treat your content like any standard, hardcoded dataset. 1. Store Policies (Google Play & Apple App Store) Generative AI Policies: Store rules around Generative AI (like Google Play's AI-generated content guidelines or Apple's live API disclosure requirements) apply primarily to apps that generate content on-demand via live AI models (e.g., live AI chatbots, dynamic image generators, or live translation tools). Static Content: Since your quiz content is compiled in your database and manually verified before reaching the user, store reviewers view it simply as developer-provided static content. 2. Best Practices for Your Disclaimers Instead of getting into technical details about how the data was compiled (e.g., mentioning "downloaded from AI and reviewed manually"), focus on general standard legal protections in your Terms of Service: Standard Legal Disclaimer: Keep standard language stating that content is provided "for educational and entertainment purposes on an AS IS basis" without guaranteeing 100% error-free accuracy. In-App Reporting: Providing an in-app "Report an Error" option allows users to flag typos or incorrect quiz items directly to you. can you make sure you uncluded those terms here Terms of Service - Vocabulary Daily

You may not copy, distribute, modify, reverse engineer, or create derivative works from any part of the app without prior written permission.

3. User Data & Local Progress

  • No Account Required: You do not need to create an account or provide personal login credentials to use the app.
  • Local Data Responsibility: Your streak counts, quiz results, and preferences are stored locally on your device. Uninstalling the app, clearing application cache/data, or changing devices will result in the permanent loss of local progress. We are not liable for any lost learning progress or streak data.

4. Prohibited Conduct

By using Vocabulary Daily, you agree not to:

  • Attempt to decompile, reverse-engineer, or extract the source code of the app.
  • Automate data collection, scrape, or extract vocabulary materials from our network APIs.
  • Use the application for any illegal or unauthorized purpose.
  • Interfere with or disrupt third-party services integrated into the app (e.g., ad networks or push notification services).

5. Educational Disclaimer

Vocabulary Daily is designed as a supplementary learning tool for educational and informational purposes only. While we strive for absolute accuracy in our definitions, pronunciations, and quiz questions, we make no guarantees regarding complete accuracy or specific educational outcomes (such as test scores or exam performance).

6. Third-Party Services & Advertisements

The app displays advertisements powered by Google AdMob and uses third-party analytics and notification frameworks (such as Firebase). We do not control third-party content or policies. Your interactions with third-party services integrated into the app are governed by their respective terms and privacy policies.

7. Disclaimer of Warranties

Vocabulary Daily is provided on an "AS IS" and "AS AVAILABLE" basis without warranties of any kind, whether express or implied. We do not warrant that the app will be uninterrupted, error-free, secure, or free of viruses or other harmful components.

8. Limitation of Liability

To the fullest extent permitted by law, Vocabulary Daily and its developers shall not be liable for any indirect, incidental, consequential, or punitive damages arising out of your access to, use of, or inability to use the application or any content provided therein.
